\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{15.87773pt}{-3.47223pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }{{{}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{39.12213pt}{-3.47223pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{74}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}=\begin{cases}|u\rangle\langle u|\boxtimes I\\
I\boxtimes|v\rangle\langle v|\end{cases}\,.

Moreover, in order for Eq.
(\ref{eq:not1})
to be
satisfied,
\outerproduct{u}{u}
(
\outerproduct{v}{v}
)
should be eigenoperator of the cell-wise unitary map
\mathcal{U}
(
\mathcal{V})
in
Eq.
(\ref{eq:Uswap})
. It follows that our
projection will select only the right (left)
algebra and the renormalised evolution will
consist in a right (left) shift.

\,\blacksquare

One may also wonder whether an FDFC on
\mathbb{Z}
can be renormalised to a Majorana shift. In the following section, we show that in the case of
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisations of FDFCs on
\mathbb{Z}
with a single fermionic mode per cell this cannot occur (Table
1
). We do not address the general case here, but we conjecture that for any
(N,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisations with even
N
such a possibility is excluded. On the other hand, the
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisations of Majorana shifts on
\mathbb{Z}
with a single fermionic mode per cell always change the index (Table
1
).

V
Renormalisation of one-dimensional spinless FCA

In this section, leveraging the tools developed so far, we classify the
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisations of nearest-neighbour FCA on
\mathbb{Z}
with a single (spinless) fermionic mode per cell
\mathsf{A}_{x}\cong\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
.

We refer to renormalisations shown in Fig.
4
as
trivial renormalisations
and we do not discuss them further.

In Table
1
we list all the non-trivial
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisable FCA, along with their
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalised FCA.
The detailed proofs are presented in Appendix
B
. Despite there are more renormalisable FCA than QCA,
similarly to results found for qubit chains
trezzini2024renormalisationquantumcellularautomata
, all
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisable FCA fail to propagate information except through trivial shifts. Renormalisable FCA are either factorised or possess commuting Margolus layers, and this allows to reduce multiple time steps by merging all odd (even) layers into a new Margolus scheme, to recast the dynamics as that of an effective single-step evolution with unchanged neighbourhood size. Furthermore, as noted in the remark
5
below, the projections
P_{e},P_{o}
map the fermionic algebra to a bosonic one, whereas the remaining projections in Table (
1
) preserve the fermionic nature of cells. Direct inspection of Table (
1
) shows that the only nontrivial fixed point corresponds to case
\phi=\theta=(2n\pi)/3
via projection
P_{o}
, thus ultimately describing a quantum cellular automaton of qubits. Genuine fermionic cellular automata admit only shifts as fixed points of the
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisation flow.

Table 1:

Summary of the renormalisation flow of nearest-neighbour spinless FCA on
\mathbb{Z}
.
For each FCA and its parameters, the non-trivial
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalised FCA associated with specific projections
\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}}
(or equivalently
P_{\Lambda_{0}}
) are listed.
Definitions:

P_{e}=|0\rangle\!\langle 0|\boxtimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|+|1\rangle\!\langle 1|\boxtimes|1\rangle\!\langle 1|
,

P_{o}=|0\rangle\!\langle 0|\boxtimes|1\rangle\!\langle 1|+|1\rangle\!\langle 1|\boxtimes|0\rangle\!\langle 0|
,

P_{L}=I\boxtimes|c\rangle\!\langle c|
,

P_{R}=\outerproduct{c}{c}\boxtimes I
, with
c=0,1
,

\Pi^{X}=\tfrac{1}{2}(I\boxtimes I\pm iX\boxtimes X)
,

\Pi^{Y}=\tfrac{1}{2}(I\boxtimes I\pm iY\boxtimes Y)
, and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
. Angles are defined modulo
2\pi n
.
Primed quantities denote renormalised counterparts. For
\phi=n\pi
or shifts
\tau_{\pm}
,
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisation is trivial, yielding to cell-wise unitaries and shifts, respectively, independent of
\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}}
.

Remark 5
.

The projections
P_{e,o}=\frac{1}{2}(I\boxtimes I\pm Z\boxtimes Z)
select the even (
+
) and odd (
-
) sectors of the algebra.

The coisometries
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
associated to
P_{e}=|e\rangle\!\langle e|
and
P_{o}=|o\rangle\!\langle o|
map a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra onto an ungraded one—to be precise, onto a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra whose odd sector is empty.
Indeed, as a generic coisometry associated to a rank-2 projection on
\mathsf{A}^{\Pi}(\Lambda_{x})
acts as

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(O)=(|\psi_{0}\rangle\langle a_{0}|+|\psi_{1}\rangle\langle a_{1}|)\,O\,(|a_{0}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{0}|+|a_{1}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{1}|),

where
a_{i}\in\{e,o\}

and
|\psi_{0}\rangle\!\langle e|,|\psi_{1}\rangle\!\langle o|
are even and

|\psi_{1}\rangle\!\langle e|,|\psi_{0}\rangle\!\langle o|
are odd maps,

the coarse-grained parity operator
Q_{x}^{r}=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(Q_{\Lambda_{x}})
can be one of the following:

\displaystyle Q^{r}_{x}=\begin{cases}\text{sgn}(a)\,I\quad\>\text{ if }\;a_{0}=a_{1}=a\,,\\
\text{sgn}(a)\,Z\quad\text{ if }\;a_{1}\neq a_{0}=a\,,\end{cases}

where
a\in\{e,o\}
and
\text{sgn}(e)\coloneqq+
,
\text{sgn}(o)\coloneqq-
.
Different choices of
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
map the algebra
\mathsf{A}(\Lambda_{x})
, graded by
Q_{\Lambda_{x}}
, onto an algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}_{x}
graded by
Q^{r}_{x}
.
When
Q_{x}^{r}
is proportional to the identity it commutes with all operators in the algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}_{x}
, so every element is even. This recovers the well-known result that the even sector of two fermionic modes is isomorphic to the algebra of a single bosonic mode.

VI
Conclusions

This work introduces a new framework for analysing and manipulating the large-scale behavior of fermionic cellular automata, providing both conceptual insight into the structure of discrete fermionic dynamics and practical tools for the simulation and control of those systems. Our procedure does not aim to produce a continuum field theory, but instead preserves the discrete structure. This allows us to stay entirely within a class of physically implementable, digital dynamical systems, while still probing their large-scale behavior.

In the simple case study of one-dimensional spinless FCA, we find that extending to fermionic cellular automata enlarges the zoology of renormalisable evolutions with respect to the qubit case
trezzini2024renormalisationquantumcellularautomata
, yet the same conceptual limitation remains: no renormalisable automaton propagates information in a nontrivial way. This may stem from the intrinsic simplicity of the chosen model, and one may conjecture that local algebras of larger dimension admit richer renormalisable dynamics.

Interestingly, FCA that after
N
time steps differ by composition with finite–depth fermionic circuits preserving the projection
\Pi
, are
(N,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalised to the same FCA. In this sense, those FCA belong to the same symmetry–protected phase, where the protecting symmetry is enforced by
\Pi
.

Finally, our procedure is designed as an
exact
renormalisation: no error is allowed in the comparison between the original and the renormalised evolutions. As such, it provides a rigorous baseline for developing
approximate
renormalisation schemes, where some constraints are relaxed, e.g., to obtain the best local-unitary approximation after tracing out selected degrees of freedom
Rotundo
.
Another promising avenue is to connect this work with the operator-algebraic renormalisation of lattice field theories, investigated in
PhysRevLett.127.230601
;
Osborne:2023aa
;
Luijk:2024aa
. Such generalisations could reveal richer renormalisation flows, universality classes, and continuum limits. Nevertheless, as an exact renormalisation, our approach should correctly identify the fixed points of those approximate renormalisation schemes.

Appendix A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras

Definition 11

(
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded vector space
)
.

A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
\mathbb{K}
-vector space
\mathsf{A}
is a pair

\mathsf{A}=(\mathsf{A}^{0},\mathsf{A}^{1})\coloneqq\mathsf{A}^{0}\sqcup\mathsf{A}^{1}\,,

where
\mathsf{A}^{p}
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{2}
, are two vector spaces over a field
\mathbb{K}
. The sum is defined only between elements of
\mathsf{A}
belonging to the same vector space

\displaystyle O_{1},O_{2}\in\mathsf{A}^{p},\qquad O_{1}+O_{2}\in\mathsf{A}^{p}.

Definition 12

(
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra
)
.

A unital
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
graded
\mathbb{K}
-vector space

\mathsf{A}=(\mathsf{A}^{0},\mathsf{A}^{1})\coloneqq\mathsf{A}^{0}\sqcup\mathsf{A}^{1}\,,

endowed with an associative distributive product:

\displaystyle O_{1}\in\mathsf{A}^{p},O_{2}\in\mathsf{A}^{q},\qquad O_{1}O_{2}\in\mathsf{A}^{p\oplus q},

where
\oplus
denotes sum modulo 2, and
I_{\mathsf{A}}\in\mathsf{A}^{0}
is the unit element.
If
\mathsf{A}^{1}=\{0\}
, then
\mathsf{A}=\mathsf{A}^{0}
as associative
\mathbb{K}
-algebra.

We stress that according to Definition
12
, referring to an element
O
of
\mathsf{A}
means
O\in\mathsf{A}^{p}
. We refer to the dimension of the algebra (vector space) as
\operatorname{dim}(\mathsf{A})=\operatorname{dim}(\mathsf{A}^{0})+\operatorname{dim}(\mathsf{A}^{1})
. When no ambiguity can arise, we write
I
for the unit element of the algebra.
Note that a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra is an instance of
G
-graded algebra where
(G,\circ)
is any monoid,
\mathsf{A}=\bigsqcup_{p\in G}\mathsf{A}^{p}
with
A_{p}A_{q}\in\mathsf{A}^{p\circ q}
for
A_{r}\in\mathsf{A}^{r}

[
53
]
.

Definition 13

(Parity.)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra. Defining the map
g:\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{Z}_{2}
such that
g(O)=p
for any
O\in\mathsf{A}^{p}
, we call
g(O)
the parity of the element
O
. We refer to
O
as an even operator if
g(O)=0
and as an odd operator if
g(O)=1
.

The notion of parity naturally extends to maps. Given a map between two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras
\eta:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{B}
, we call
\eta
an even map if
g(\eta(O))=g(O)
for any
O\in\mathsf{A}
and we call
\eta
an odd map if
g(\eta(O))=g(O)\oplus 1
for any
O\in\mathsf{A}
.

Definition 14

(
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded subalgebra)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras. We say that
\mathsf{B}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded subalgebra of
\mathsf{A}
if

\mathsf{B}^{p}=\mathsf{B}\cap\mathsf{A}^{p}.

In this case we write
\mathsf{B}\subseteq\mathsf{A}
.

Definition 15

(Graded morphism)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras,
O_{i}\in\mathsf{A}
, and
c_{i}\in\mathbb{K}
. A graded morphism
\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{B}
acts as
\mathcal{M}(c_{1}O_{1}+c_{2}O_{2}O_{3})=c_{1}\mathcal{M}(O_{1})+c_{2}\mathcal{M}(O_{2})\mathcal{M}(O_{3})
with
\mathcal{M}(\mathsf{A}^{p})\subseteq\mathsf{B}^{p}
. Hence
g(\mathcal{M}(O))=g(O)
for any
O\in\mathsf{A}
.

Definition 16

(Graded commutator)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra and let
O_{1},O_{2}\in\mathsf{A}
.
The graded commutator is

\{[O_{1},O_{2}]\}\coloneqq O_{1}O_{2}-(-1)^{g(O_{1})g(O_{2})}O_{2}O_{1}.

The graded commutator reduces to the anticommutator if
O_{1},O_{2}
are both odd elements,
and to the commutator otherwise.

Definition 17

(Graded center)
.

The graded center
\mathsf{Z}(\mathsf{A})
of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded subalgebra of
\mathsf{A}
given by all the elements of
\mathsf{A}
that graded commute with any element of
\mathsf{A}
. The algebra
\mathsf{A}
is called central if
\mathsf{Z}(\mathsf{A})\cap\mathsf{A}^{0}=\mathbb{K}
.

Definition 18

(Simple)
.

A two-sided ideal
\mathsf{I}(\mathsf{A})
of a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra
\mathsf{A}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded subalgebra of
\mathsf{A}
such that
AO,OA\in\mathsf{I}(\mathsf{A})
for any
A\in\mathsf{A}
and
O\in\mathsf{I}
.
A
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra
\mathsf{A}
is called simple if the only two-sided ideals of
\mathsf{A}
are
\{0\}
and
\mathsf{A}
.

Definition 19

(Graded tensor product)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
two
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras. The graded tensor product algebra
\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B}
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra given by

\begin{split}&\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B}=((\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B})^{0},(\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B})^{1}),\\
&(\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B})^{r}\coloneqq\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}p,q=0,1\\
p\oplus q=r\end{subarray}}\mathsf{A}^{p}\otimes\mathsf{B}^{q},\end{split}

where
\otimes
is the standard tensor product of
\mathbb{K}
-vector spaces. For every
A\in\mathsf{A}
and
B\in\mathsf{B}
we denote
A\boxtimes B
the corresponding element of
\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B}

and
for any
A_{i}\in\mathsf{A}
,
B_{i}\in\mathsf{B}

\displaystyle(A_{1}\boxtimes B_{1})(A_{2}\boxtimes B_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{g(B_{1})g(A_{2})}(A_{1}A_{2}\boxtimes B_{1}B_{2})\,.

(47)

Remark 6
.

As a consequence of (
47
) the graded tensor product has the following braiding relation

\displaystyle\{[(A\boxtimes I_{\mathsf{B}}),(I_{\mathsf{A}}\boxtimes B)]\}=0\,.

Remark 7
.

By definition,
g(I)=0
, and consequently for any
A_{i}\in\mathsf{A}
and
A\boxtimes B\in\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B}
we have
g(A_{1}A_{2})=g(A_{1})\oplus g(A_{2})
,
g(A\boxtimes B)=g(A)\oplus g(B)
.

As in the ungraded case, equipping a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra with a suitable norm and an involution, we get a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra.

Definition 20

(
C^{*}
-algebra)
.

A
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathsf{A}
is an associative
\mathbb{C}
-algebra decorated with an involution
\dagger
and a norm
\norm{\cdot}
, such that it is complete in the metric induced by the norm,
\norm{AB}\leq\norm{A}\norm{B}
, and
\norm{A^\dagger A}=\norm{A}^{2}
for all
A,B\in\mathsf{A}
.

Definition 21

(
*
-morphism)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
be
C^{*}
-algebras,
O_{i}\in\mathsf{A}
, and
c_{i}\in\mathbb{K}
. A
*
-morphism
\mathcal{M}:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{B}
acts as
\mathcal{M}(c_{1}O_{1}+c_{2}O_{2}^{\dagger}O_{3})=c_{1}\mathcal{M}(O_{1})+c_{2}\mathcal{M}(O_{2})^{\dagger}\mathcal{M}(O_{3})
.

Remark 8
.

If
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
are
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras with involution
{\dagger}
, then
\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B}
is a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra with the natural inherited
C^{*}
structure. To be precise, in general one must specify a
C^{*}
-norm (minimal, maximal, etc.) on the algebraic tensor product
\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B}
. In the finite-dimensional case all such norms coincide, so the distinction is immaterial
[
54
]
. In particular, we choose a norm on the tensor product algebra such that
\norm{A\gt I}=\norm{A}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\norm{I\gt B}=\norm{B}_{\mathsf{B}}
for any
A\in\mathsf{A}
and
B\in\mathsf{B}
. Let
A\boxtimes B\in\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{B}
, we highlight that

\displaystyle(A\boxtimes B)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=(I_{\mathsf{A}}\boxtimes B)^{\dagger}(A\boxtimes I_{\mathsf{B}})^{\dagger}=

\displaystyle=(-1)^{g(B)g(A)}(A^{\dagger}\boxtimes B^{\dagger})\,.

Definition 22

(Generators)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra. We refer to elements
\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n}\in\mathsf{A}
as generators of
\mathsf{A}
if they form a minimal set such that every element of
\mathsf{A}
can be expressed as a linear combination of products of the
\gamma_{j}
. In this case, we write
\mathsf{A}=\expectationvalue{\gamma_1,\ldots,\gamma_n}
.

Definition 23

(Notable algebras)
.

For,
\mathbb{N}\ni p,q<\infty
, the matrix algebra
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
\mathbb{C}
-algebra defined as

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})\coloneqq\mathbb{C}^{p^{2}+q^{2}}\sqcup\mathbb{C}^{2pq}\,,

with
\mathbb{C}^{0}\coloneqq\{0\}
. The Clifford algebra
\mathcal{C}\ell_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
\mathbb{C}
-algebra defined as

\mathcal{C}\ell_{n}(\mathbb{C})\coloneqq\mathbb{C}^{2^{n-1}}\sqcup\mathbb{C}^{2^{n-1}}\,,

with
\mathbb{N}^{+}\ni n<\infty
.

Notice that
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert 0})=\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{0\lvert p})=\text{Mat}_{p\times p}(\mathbb{C})
is the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra of complex
p\times p
matrices. Moreover, the Clifford algebra
\mathcal{C}\ell_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is the
n
-fold graded tensor product of
\mathcal{C}\ell_{1}(\mathbb{C})
, and when
n=2k
is even then
\mathcal{C}\ell_{2k}(\mathbb{C})=\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{2^{k-1}\lvert 2^{k-1}})
. By
\mathcal{C}\ell_{1}^{\gamma}(\mathbb{C})=\expectationvalue{\gamma}
we denote the algebra
\mathcal{C}\ell_{1}(\mathbb{C})
specifying an odd generator
\gamma
(note that necessarily
\gamma^{2}=I
).

Definition 24

(Superselected state)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra.
A state is a linear map
\omega:\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{C}
with
\omega(A^{\dagger}A)\geq 0
for all
A\in\mathsf{A}
, and
\omega(I_{\mathsf{A}})=1
. A superselected state is a state such that
\omega(\mathsf{A}^{p})\subseteq\mathbb{C}^{p\oplus 1}
.

Definition 25

(Tracial state)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra.
The tracial state of
\mathsf{A}
is a superselected state
\operatorname{Tr}:\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{C}
such that
\operatorname{Tr}(AB)=\operatorname{Tr}(BA)
for any
A,B\in\mathsf{A}
.

Definition 26

(Basis)
.

A basis of
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
is a set of elements
B_{ij}\in\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
with
B_{ij}B_{kl}=\delta_{jk}B_{il}
and
B_{ij}^{\dagger}=B_{ji}
, which form a basis of
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
as a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded vector space.
As a consequence
i,j=1,\ldots,p+q
,
g(B_{ij})=g(B_{ji})
,
B_{ii}
are even, and the number of odd basis elements is
2pq
.

In the body of the manuscript we use the notation
|i\rangle\!\langle j|\coloneqq B_{ij}
.

Remark 9
.

\mathsf{A}=\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q}),\mathcal{C}\ell_{n}(\mathbb{C})
are both
C^{*}
-algebras endowed with the operator norm
\norm{O}\coloneqq\sup_{\omega}\sqrt{\omega(O^{\dagger}O)}
, where
O\in\mathsf{A}
and
\omega
superselected states.

For the algebra
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
, a state
\omega
is superselected if and only if
\omega(O)=\omega(Q\,O\,Q^{\dagger})
for all
O\in\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
, where
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})\ni Q\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{p}\outerproduct{i}{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{q}\outerproduct{p+j}{p+j}
is called the parity element.

Remark 10
.

A common way to introduce
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras in the literature is through direct sums of vector spaces. Although this definition is algebraically equivalent, we adopt a formulation based on disjoint unions because it makes the superselection structure manifest. In physical terms, the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
–grading distinguishes superselection sectors corresponding to even (bosonic) and odd (fermionic) elements, which cannot be coherently superposed.
In discussing matrix algebras
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
, the vector spaces
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})^{0},\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})^{1}
can be represented as

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})^{0}\cong

\displaystyle\cong\left\{\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{pmatrix}\;:\;A\in\text{Mat}_{p\times p}(\mathbb{C})\,,B\in\text{Mat}_{q\times q}(\mathbb{C})\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})^{1}\cong

\displaystyle\cong\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0&C\\
D&0\end{pmatrix}\;:\;C\in\text{Mat}_{p\times q}(\mathbb{C})\,,D\in\text{Mat}_{q\times p}(\mathbb{C})\right\}\,.

In this case, the parity element of the algebra is represented by

\displaystyle Q=\begin{pmatrix}I_{p\times p}&\\
&-I_{q\times q}\end{pmatrix}\,.

We stress that in this representation the basis elements
|i\rangle\!\langle j|
are those per Definition
26
, and should not be confused with ungraded matrix entries as they obey graded commutation relations.

Definition 27

(Idempotent)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebra. We refer to
P\in\mathsf{A}
as idempotent if
P^{2}=P
. A primitive idempotent
E\in\mathsf{A}
is an idempotent such that
E=Q+R
implies either
Q=0
or
R=0
.

Any idempotent is an even element of the algebra, and can be decomposed as the sum of primitive idempotents. The basis elements
B_{ii}
of
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
are primitive idempotents. Viceversa, given
p+q
orthogonal primitive idempotents
E_{i}E_{j}=\delta_{ij}E_{i}
, there exists a basis
B_{ij}
such that
E_{i}=B_{ii}
.

Definition 28

(Projection)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra. A projection
\Pi\in\mathsf{A}
is an idempotent of
\mathsf{A}
such that
\Pi^{\dagger}=\Pi
. As such,
\Pi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}E_{i}
with
E_{i}\in\mathsf{A}
primitive idempotents. The rank of the projection
\Pi
is given by
\text{rank}(\Pi)\coloneqq r
.

Since projections can always be decomposed in terms of primitive idempotents as
\Pi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}B_{ii}
, we can write projections as
\Pi=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\outerproduct{i}{i}
. The element

I-\Pi
is also a projection, and if
\Pi\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}\Pi
and
\operatorname{rank}(\Pi)=\operatorname{rank}(\Pi^{\prime})
then
\Pi=\Pi^{\prime}
.

Remark 11
.

Let
\mathsf{A}\cong\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\cong\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p^{\prime}\lvert q^{\prime}})
,
\Pi\in\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
a projection, and
E_{i}\in\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
,
D_{i}\in\mathsf{A}
,
D^{\prime}_{i}\in\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
primitive idempotents. As in general
E_{i}\neq\sum_{kl}d_{kl}D_{k}\boxtimes D^{\prime}_{l}
, instead of using primitive idempotents, we often conveniently write
\Pi
in a basis of
\mathsf{A}\boxtimes\mathsf{A}^{\prime}

\Pi=\sum_{ijkl}c_{ijkl}B_{ij}\boxtimes B^{\prime}_{kl}
where
B_{ij}
and
B_{kl}
are a basis of
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
respectively,
d_{kl},c_{ijkl}\in\mathbb{C}
.

Definition 29

(Unitary elements)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a unital
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra. An element
U\in\mathsf{A}
is
unitary
if
U^{\dagger}U=UU^{\dagger}=I_{\mathsf{A}}

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a unital
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra,
\Pi\in\mathsf{A}
be a projection and
U\in\mathsf{A}
a unitary. Then
U\Pi U^{\dagger}
is a projection, and
\operatorname{rank}(U\Pi U^{\dagger})=\operatorname{rank}(\Pi)
.

Definition 30
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra, and let
\Pi,\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{A}
be projections. We define the ordering
\Pi\geq\Pi^{\prime}
if
\Pi\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}
.

Definition 31
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra, and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{A}
a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-subalgebra. We say that a projection
\Pi\in\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
is
maximal
in
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{A}
if, for every projection
\Pi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{A}
such that
\Pi^{\prime}\geq\Pi
, one has
\Pi^{\prime}\not\in\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
.

Definition 32

(Isometries)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}\cong\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p\lvert q})
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\cong\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p^{\prime}\lvert q^{\prime}})
with
p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}\leq p+q
, and choose any basis
B_{ij}
and
B^{\prime}_{ij}
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{J}_{i^{\prime}i,j^{\prime}j}:\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{A}
with
i,j=1,\ldots,p+q
and
i^{\prime},j^{\prime}=1,\ldots,p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}
be maps such that
\mathcal{J}_{i^{\prime}i,j^{\prime}j}(B^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}l^{\prime}})=\delta_{i^{\prime}k^{\prime}}\delta_{j^{\prime}l^{\prime}}B_{ij}
. Let
H=\{1,2,\ldots,p^{\prime}+q^{\prime}\}
, and
f:H\to\{1,2,\ldots,p+q\}
be injective. The map

\mathcal{E}=\sum_{k,l\in H}\mathcal{J}_{lf(l),kf(k)}

is an
isometry
.
\mathcal{E}
is a
*
-homomorphism, and it is a
*
-isomorphism if
\operatorname{dim}(\mathsf{A})=\operatorname{dim}(\mathsf{A}^{\prime})
.

Remark 12
.

For convenience, we use the formal notation

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{lf(l),kf(k)}(X)=|f(l)\rangle\!\langle l|X|k\rangle\!\langle f(k)|,

even though
|f(l)\rangle\!\langle l|
,
|l\rangle\!\langle f(l)|
,
are not rigorously defined as standalone objects. Their use
in this respect is completely analogous to the use of bras and kets.

Definition 33
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
be finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras, and let
\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathsf{A}}
and
\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}
denote tracial states for
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{J}:\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{A}
be an even linear map. Then its dual
\mathcal{J}^{\dagger}:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
is the linear map implicitly defined by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}[\mathcal{J}^{\dagger}(Y)X]\coloneqq\operatorname{Tr}_{\mathsf{A}}[Y\mathcal{J}(X)].

(48)

One can straightforwardly prove that the dual of an even linear map is well defined and unique, and that
(\mathcal{J}^{\dagger})^{\dagger}=\mathcal{J}
. The following lemma holds.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}
be as in Definition
32
. Then
\mathcal{J}_{i^{\prime}i,j^{\prime}j}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{J}_{jj^{\prime},ii^{\prime}}
.

Remark 13
.

Notice that, by definition, we have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{lf(l),kf(k)}(X)\mathcal{J}_{l^{\prime}f(l^{\prime}),k^{\prime}f(k^{\prime})}(Y)=

(49)

\displaystyle\quad=\delta_{kl^{\prime}}\mathcal{J}_{lf(l),k^{\prime}f(k^{\prime})}(X|k\rangle\!\langle k|Y),

(50)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}^{\dagger}_{lf(l),kf(k)}(X)\mathcal{J}^{\dagger}_{l^{\prime}f(l^{\prime}),k^{\prime}f(k^{\prime})}(Y)=

(51)

\displaystyle\quad=\delta_{k^{\prime}l}\mathcal{J}^{\dagger}_{l^{\prime}f(l^{\prime}),kf(k)}(X|k^{\prime}\rangle\!\langle k^{\prime}|Y).

(52)

Definition 34
.

Let
\mathcal{E}:\mathsf{A}^{\prime}\to\mathsf{A}
be an isometry. Then
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
is a
coisometry
.

From the above definition one can check that if
\mathcal{E}
is an isometry, then
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(I_{\mathsf{A}})=I_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}}
and
\mathcal{E}(I_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}})=\Pi
, where
\Pi\in\mathsf{A}
is a projection.

Definition 35
.

(Graded direct sum)
Let
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras, then

\mathsf{A}\boxplus\mathsf{B}\coloneqq(\mathsf{A}^{0}\oplus\mathsf{B}^{0},\mathsf{A}^{1}\oplus\mathsf{B}^{1})\,,

where
\oplus
is the standard direct sum of vector spaces.

The fundamental Theorem of finite-dimensional associative algebras, also known as Wedderburn-Artin Theorem, can be generalised to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded algebras as follows.

Theorem 2

(
[
40
]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}
be a finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded semisimple
\mathbb{C}
-algebra, then

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}\cong\bigboxplus_{i}\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{p_{i}\lvert q_{i}})\boxtimes D^{m_{i}}

where
D^{m_{i}}=(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{C}^{m_{i}})
, and
m_{i}\in\{0,1\}
. If the index set of
i
is larger than one, then
\mathsf{A}
is called semi-simple.

As every finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra is semisimple, the theorem applies in this case, which is of interest to us.
Thanks to the graded generalization of the Wedderburn-Artin theorem, it is possible to show that another fundamental result, the Skolem–Noether Theorem, also extends naturally to graded algebras.

Theorem 3

(
[
53
]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{A}\subseteq\mathsf{B}
be
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded central simple
C^{*}
-algebras. Let
\varphi:\mathsf{A}\to\mathsf{B}
an injective graded
*
-morphism. Then there exists a unitary element
U\in\mathsf{B}
such that
\varphi(O)=U^{\dagger}OU
for any
O\in\mathsf{A}
.

Appendix B
Renormalisation of one-dimensional spinless FCA

In this appendix, we apply the propositions presented in the body of the manuscipt to the
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisation of FCA on
\mathbb{Z}
with a single (spinless) fermionic mode per cell.
Firstly, we examine FCA with index
1
, i.e., those FCA that can be realised as FDFCs. Given the FCA
\mathcal{T}
—equivalently the associated gate
\mathcal{G}
(Eq. (
24
))—we identify for which gate parameters and projections
\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}}
—equivalently the associated projections
P
(Eq. (
23
))—
\mathcal{T}
is
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisable to an FCA
\mathcal{S}
, which then will be explicitly computed. Secondly, we study the necessary and sufficient conditions for
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisation of Majorana shifts, and compute the renormalised FCA for those cases where they exist.

In view of their role in Eq. (
33
) for the renormalisability of FDFCs (Prop.
2
), we list the subalgebras of
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
introducing a lighter notation for the following analysis.

Lemma 6
.

The
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-subalgebras of
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})\cong\mathcal{C}\ell_{2}(\mathbb{C})
are

1.

\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
: the matrix algebra itself,

2.

\mathcal{C}\ell^{\gamma}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
: a Clifford algebra generated by an odd element
\gamma
,

3.

\mathcal{A}\coloneqq\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})^{0}
: the abelian algebra generated by the Majorana element
Z
,

4.

\mathbb{C}\mathcal{I}\coloneqq\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 0})
: the trivial algebra of complex scalars.

In the following we denote the cosets sets of unitary elements of
\mathsf{A}^{p}_{x}
as
SU^{p}(2)
with
p=0,1
.

B.1
Schumacher-Werner FCA

In the case of Schumacher-Werner FCA (Eq. (
6
)), the fermionic gate
\mathcal{G}
(Eq. (
24
)) retains the same formal structure as in the qubit setting
[
18
]
, but expressed with Majorana modes:

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.44 11.81 L 0.06 11.81 C -7.58 11.81 -13.78 5.62 -13.78 -2.03 L -13.78 2.03 C -13.78 -5.62 -7.58 -11.81 0.06 -11.81 L 31.44 -11.81 C 39.08 -11.81 45.28 -5.62 45.28 2.03 L 45.28 -2.03 C 45.28 5.62 39.08 11.81 31.44 11.81 Z M -13.78 -11.81}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.5547pt}{-2.66666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}={\hbox to43.68pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.45845pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 19.69 L 31.5 -19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 19.69 L 0 -19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.44 11.81 L 0.06 11.81 C -7.58 11.81 -13.78 5.62 -13.78 -2.03 L -13.78 2.03 C -13.78 -5.62 -7.58 -11.81 0.06 -11.81 L 31.44 -11.81 C 39.08 -11.81 45.28 -5.62 45.28 2.03 L 45.28 -2.03 C 45.28 5.62 39.08 11.81 31.44 11.81 Z M -13.78 -11.81}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{5.11583pt}{-1.98611pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ ,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{M}_{1}\mathcal{M}_{2}=\mathcal{C}_{\phi}\,(\mathcal{U}\boxtimes\mathcal{U})\,\mathcal{C}_{\phi},

with two admissible forms taking into account
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G=C_{\phi}(e^{i\theta Z}\boxtimes e^{i\theta Z})C_{\phi}\,,\quad\text{ or}\\
&G=C_{\phi}\,[\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{M}\boxtimes\mathbf{n}\cdot\mathbf{M}]\,C_{\phi}\,,\end{aligned}

(53)

with
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
,
n_{2}/n_{1}=\tan\theta\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathbf{M}=(X,Y)
. Recall that
\mathcal{G}(\cdot)=G^{\dagger}(\cdot)G
.
Interestingly, the forms of
G
in the superselected qubit case are exactly those that are known to support
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisation in the qubit framework
[
18
]
. This leads to the following result.

Lemma 7
.

All Schumacher-Werner FCA satisfy Eq. (
33
) with
\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N}=\mathcal{A}
. Additionally, if
G=C_{\frac{n\pi}{2}}\,({U}\boxtimes{U})\,C_{\frac{n\pi}{2}}
with
{U}\in SU^{0,1}(2)
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
then Eq. (
33
) holds with
\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N}=\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
.

Proof.

Parity superselection restricts the form of
G
to that given in Eq. (
53
), i.e.,
U
has a fixed parity. According to the analysis in
[
18
]
, such evolutions are precisely those that preserve factorised elements in a suitable subalgebra. However, within the fermionic setting, we need to differentiate between preserving factorised elements of an abelian even algebra and those in a factorised Clifford algebra.

Recall that
\outerproduct{0}{0}U=U\outerproduct{1}{1}
for every odd
U
. We can rewrite
G
as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G=C_{\phi}\,(U\boxtimes U)\,C_{\phi}=(U\boxtimes U)\,C_{2\phi},\quad\text{if }U\in SU^{0}(2),\\
&G=C_{\phi}\,(U\boxtimes U)\,C_{\phi}=(U\boxtimes U)\,\widetilde{C}_{2\phi},\quad\text{if }U\in SU^{1}(2),\end{aligned}

(54)

where we recall that
C_{\phi}
can be represented by

\displaystyle C_{\phi}=|0\rangle\langle 0|\boxtimes I+|1\rangle\langle 1|\boxtimes e^{i\frac{\phi}{2}(I-Z)},

and the modified elements are defined as

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{\beta}=|0\rangle\langle 0|\boxtimes e^{i\frac{\beta}{2}(I+Z)}+|1\rangle\langle 1|\boxtimes e^{i\frac{\beta}{2}(I-Z)}.

In both scenarios (
54
), we observe that any factorised element
Z^{a}\boxtimes Z^{b}
remains factorised, as both
C_{\phi}
and
U\boxtimes U
preserve it.
If
G
also maps factorised elements in the Clifford algebras
\mathcal{C}\ell^{\xi}_{1}(\mathbb{C})\boxtimes\mathcal{C}\ell^{\eta}_{1}(\mathbb{C})
to factorised elements, for any odd elements
\xi,\eta
, then it necessarily maps factorised elements to factorised elements. Indeed, if of both
Z\boxtimes I
and
\xi\boxtimes I
are mapped to factorised elements, also
Z\xi\boxtimes I
, is mapped to a factorised element. Similarly for elements of the form
I\boxtimes Z
,
I\boxtimes\eta
and
I\boxtimes Z\eta
.
By Lemma (
3
), in this case
G
must take the form

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G=(U\boxtimes V)\,H^{b}\,,\quad b=0,1\\
&H=\outerproduct{0}{0}\boxtimes I+\outerproduct{1}{1}\boxtimes Z\,.\end{aligned}

Therefore

\phi=\frac{k\pi}{2}
,
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

\,\blacksquare

From the previous lemma, we recover the same results as in the qubit case
[
18
]
when considering projections in the

Z
-basis, i.e., when
\mathsf{M},\mathsf{N}\cong\mathcal{A}
. This case was extensively discussed in
[
18
]
. In addition, in the fermionic setting there exists an FCA that preserves a factorised matrix algebra. In the following we focus on this novel case, recalling that Eq. (
33
) is a necessary but not sufficient condition for
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisation. We have the following:

Lemma 8
.

For Schumacher-Werner FCA where

\displaystyle G=C_{\frac{n\pi}{2}}\,(U\boxtimes U)\,C_{\frac{n\pi}{2}},

(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisation is possible with projections in the
Z
-basis.

Proof.

We know from Ref.
[
18
]
that these FCA are
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisable with

\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}}=\sum_{k,j}c_{kj}Z^{k}\boxtimes Z^{j}\,,

(55)

where
k,j=0,1
and
Z^{0}=I
. Therefore it is only left to show that
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisability is not possible for other projections. Assume
\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}}
to be different from Eq. (
55
). Then
\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}}
must contain an element
\xi\boxtimes\eta
in its Schmidt decomposition with
\xi,\eta\in\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})^{1}
odd elements. In the present case, the algebras
\mathsf{M}
and
\mathsf{N}
defined in Corollary
3
are unital. Then by Corollary
3
it should hold
\mathcal{G}(I\boxtimes\xi)=I\boxtimes\widetilde{\xi}
for some odd
\widetilde{\xi}
. However, for the present FCA
\mathcal{G}(I\boxtimes\xi)=E\boxtimes\widetilde{\xi}
with a suitable even element
E\neq I
, as can be easily checked using Eqs.(
54
).

\,\blacksquare

B.2
Forking FCA

For the
Forking Automaton
(Eq.(
7
)) we have

\displaystyle{\hbox to43.68pt{\vbox to28.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.45845pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 -19.69 L 31.5 19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{4.5547pt}{-2.66666pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}={\hbox to43.68pt{\vbox to48.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.45845pt\lower-14.42638pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 29.53 L 31.5 -19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.5 19.69 L 31.5 -19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 19.69 L 0 -19.69}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 31.44 11.81 L 0.06 11.81 C -7.58 11.81 -13.78 5.62 -13.78 -2.03 L -13.78 2.03 C -13.78 -5.62 -7.58 -11.81 0.06 -11.81 L 31.44 -11.81 C 39.08 -11.81 45.28 -5.62 45.28 2.03 L 45.28 -2.03 C 45.28 5.62 39.08 11.81 31.44 11.81 Z M -13.78 -11.81}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.91753pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{66}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\ .

The main result of this section is the following.

Proposition 4
.

The Forking automata that admit a non-trivial
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisation are those with unitaries given by

\displaystyle U=\begin{cases}\exp{\left[i\left(\frac{(1+2n)\pi}{4} Z\right)\right]},\;\text{or}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(X\pm Y).\end{cases}

Those FCA are
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalised to right and left shifts by choosing the projections

\displaystyle P_{R}=\frac{1}{2}\{(I\pm Z)\boxtimes I\}\,,\;P_{L}=\frac{1}{2}\{I\boxtimes(I\pm Z)\},

respectively.

The proof of Proposition
4
is given in the following split in a few lemmas.

The
G
element associated to the gate
\mathcal{G}
associated to the Forking automaton is

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G=S_{X,Y}(U\boxtimes U)S_{X,Y}=e^{\frac{\pi}{4}X\boxtimes Y}(U\boxtimes U)e^{\frac{\pi}{4}X\boxtimes Y}=\\
&=\frac{1}{2}(I\boxtimes I+X\boxtimes Y)(U\boxtimes U)(I\boxtimes I+X\boxtimes Y)=\\
&=\frac{1}{2}[U\boxtimes U+(X\boxtimes Y)(U\boxtimes U)+\\
&+(U\boxtimes U)(X\boxtimes Y)+(X\boxtimes Y)(U\boxtimes U)(X\boxtimes Y)].\end{aligned}

Lemma 9
.

For Forking automata

\displaystyle G=\begin{cases}G_{even}\coloneqq iG_{+}\,(Z\boxtimes I)\quad\text{if}\quad U=e^{i\theta Z}\in SU^{0}(2)\\
G_{odd}\coloneqq G_{-}\quad\text{if}\quad U=\sin(\theta)X+\cos(\theta)Y\in SU^{1}(2)\end{cases}

with

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G_{\pm}=&\pm\sin\theta\cos\theta(I\boxtimes I+Z\boxtimes Z)+\\
&\mp\sin^{2}\theta(X\boxtimes X)\mp\cos^{2}\theta(Y\boxtimes Y)\,.\\
\end{aligned}

Proof.

Since
U\in SU^{0,1}(2)
has definite parity, we have
(U\boxtimes I)(I\boxtimes X)=(-1)^{g(U)g(X)}(I\boxtimes X)(U\boxtimes I)=(-1)^{g(U)}(I\boxtimes X)(U\boxtimes I)
. Then we can write
G
as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G=&\frac{1}{2}[U\boxtimes U+\\
&+(-1)^{g(U)}(XU\boxtimes YU)+\\
&+(-1)^{g(U)}(UX\boxtimes UY)+\\
&-XUX\boxtimes YUY].\end{aligned}

(56)

Inserting

U=e^{i\theta Z}\in SU^{0}(2)

in the above expression of
G
we get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G=\frac{1}{2}[e^{i\theta Z}\boxtimes e^{i\theta Z}-Xe^{i\theta Z}X\boxtimes Ye^{i\theta Z}Y+\\
&+\{X\boxtimes Y,e^{i\theta Z}\boxtimes e^{i\theta Z}\}]=\\
&=\frac{1}{2}[e^{i\theta Z}\boxtimes e^{i\theta Z}-e^{-i\theta Z}\boxtimes e^{-i\theta Z}+\\
&+2\cos^{2}(\theta)X\boxtimes Y-2\sin^{2}(\theta)Y\boxtimes X]=\\
&=i\sin\theta\cos\theta(Z\boxtimes I+I\boxtimes Z)+\\
&+\cos^{2}(\theta)X\boxtimes Y-\sin^{2}(\theta)Y\boxtimes X.\end{aligned}

We can further simplify
G
by rewriting it as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G=i[\sin\theta\cos\theta(I\boxtimes I+Z\boxtimes Z)+\\
&-\cos^{2}\theta Y\boxtimes Y-\sin^{2}\theta X\boxtimes X](Z\boxtimes I)\eqqcolon\\
&=iG_{+}\,(Z\boxtimes I).\end{aligned}

On the other hand, if we insert

U=\gamma\in SU^{1}(2)
,
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G=&\frac{1}{2}[\gamma\boxtimes\gamma+\\
&+(-1)(X\gamma\boxtimes Y\gamma)+\\
&+(-1)(\gamma X\boxtimes\gamma Y)+\\
&-X\gamma X\boxtimes Y\gamma Y].\end{aligned}

Writing
\gamma=aX+bY
with suitable
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
, we obtain

\displaystyle G

\displaystyle=a^{2}X\boxtimes X+b^{2}Y\boxtimes Y-ab(I\boxtimes I+Z\boxtimes Z)

(57)

\displaystyle=G_{-}\,.

By unitarity of
\gamma
, we have
a=e^{i\phi}\sin\theta
,
b=e^{i\phi}\cos\theta
, with
\phi,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
.

\,\blacksquare

The following Lemma holds.

Lemma 10
.

Consider
U=e^{i\theta Z}\in SU^{0}(2)
, then Eq. (
33
) is satisfied
iff

\theta=\frac{n\pi}{4}
. If this is the case, we have
\mathsf{M},\,\mathsf{N},\,\widetilde{\mathsf{M}},\,\widetilde{\mathsf{N}}=\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
.

Proof.

Let us compute the action of
\mathcal{G}
on the generators of any possible subalgebra of
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})\boxtimes\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
. We have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G_{+}^{\dagger}(\Sigma\boxtimes I)G_{+}=\\
&[\cos^{4}\theta(Y\Sigma Y)+\sin^{4}\theta(X\Sigma X)]\boxtimes I+\\
&-\cos\theta\sin^{3}\theta(\{\Sigma,X\}\boxtimes X+i\{\Sigma Z,X\}\boxtimes Y)+\\
&-\cos\theta^{3}\sin\theta(\{\Sigma,Y\}\boxtimes Y+i\{\Sigma Z,Y\}\boxtimes X)\,,\\
\\
&G_{+}^{\dagger}(I\boxtimes\Sigma)G_{+}=-\text{SWAP}(\mathcal{G}(\Sigma\boxtimes I))=\\
&-I\boxtimes[\cos^{4}\theta(Y\Sigma Y)+\sin^{4}\theta(X\Sigma X)]+\\
&+\cos\theta\sin^{3}\theta(X\boxtimes\{\Sigma,X\}+iY\boxtimes\{\Sigma Z,X\})+\\
&+\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta(Y\boxtimes\{\Sigma,Y\}+iX\boxtimes\{\Sigma Z,Y\})\,,\\
\end{aligned}

with
\Sigma=X,Y
.
Evaluating the results for the different values of
\Sigma
we get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G_{+}^{\dagger}(X\boxtimes I)G_{+}=&-\{\cos^{4}\theta-\sin^{4}\theta\}X\boxtimes I+\\
&-2(\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta+\cos\theta\sin^{3}\theta)I\boxtimes X\,,\\
G_{+}^{\dagger}(Y\boxtimes I)G_{+}=&-\{\sin^{4}\theta-\cos^{4}\theta\}Y\boxtimes I+\\
&-2(\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta-\cos\theta\sin^{3}\theta)I\boxtimes Y\,,\\
G_{+}^{\dagger}(I\boxtimes X)G_{+}=&-\{\sin^{4}\theta-\cos^{4}\theta\}I\boxtimes X+\\
&+2(\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta+\cos\theta\sin^{3}\theta)X\boxtimes I\,,\\
G_{+}^{\dagger}(I\boxtimes Y)G_{+}=&-\{\cos^{4}\theta-\sin^{4}\theta\}I\boxtimes Y+\\
&+2(\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta-\cos\theta\sin^{3}\theta)Y\boxtimes I\,.\end{aligned}

Note that
\cos^{4}\theta-\sin^{4}\theta=\cos 2\theta
,
\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta+\cos\theta\sin^{3}\theta=(1/2)\sin 2\theta
, and
\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta-\cos\theta\sin^{3}\theta=(1/4)\sin 4\theta
.
For the images of
X\boxtimes I,I\boxtimes X
to be factorised, it is necessary that

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{(2n+1)\pi}{4}\lor\theta=\frac{n\pi}{2},

while for the images of
Y\boxtimes I,I\boxtimes Y
we need

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{(2n+1)\pi}{4}\lor\theta=\frac{n\pi}{4}.

The two sets of conditions have the common set of solutions for

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{n\pi}{4}\,.

(58)

There exists no value of
\theta
for which only one generator, but not the others, is kept factorised. Hence, if the factorisation condition is met, then it is met for a matrix algebra
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
. The argument holds for any choice of generators. Indeed for a generic odd element
\eta=aX+bY
with
a,b\in\mathbb{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &G_{+}^{\dagger}(\eta\boxtimes I)G_{+}=aG_{+}^{\dagger}(X\boxtimes I)G_{+}+bG_{+}^{\dagger}(Y\boxtimes I)G_{+}=\\
&=-\cos 2\theta(aX-bY)\boxtimes I+\\
&-I\boxtimes(a\sin 2\theta X+\frac{b}{2}\sin 4\theta Y),\\
&\\
&G_{+}^{\dagger}(I\boxtimes\eta)G_{+}=\\
&=-\cos 2\theta I\boxtimes(-aX+bY)+\\
&+(a\sin 2\theta X+\frac{b}{2}\sin 4\theta Y)\boxtimes I,\end{aligned}

which lead the same factorisation conditions found in Eq. (
58
), as one can expect due to the linear independence of
\mathcal{G}(X\boxtimes I)
and
\mathcal{G}(Y\boxtimes I)
(
\mathcal{G}(I\boxtimes X)
and
\mathcal{G}(I\boxtimes Y)
).
Finally, we check if Eq. (
58
) is the only solution that keeps factorised the
Z
-generators. Let us consider

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G_{+}^{\dagger}(Z\boxtimes I)G_{+}=&i\,G_{+}^{\dagger}(Y\boxtimes I)G_{+}G_{+}^{\dagger}(X\boxtimes I)G_{+}=\\
&\cos^{2}2\theta(Z\boxtimes I)+\\
&+i\frac{1}{2}\sin 4\theta[(Y\boxtimes X)+\\
&+\cos 2\theta(X\boxtimes Y)+\\
&+i\sin 2\theta(I\boxtimes Z)].\end{aligned}

Given the independence of each subspace, the only way to have three coefficients out of four equal to zero is again to have

\theta=\frac{n\pi}{4}
.

This concludes the proof as Eq. (
33
) requires
\mathcal{G}
to map a factorised algebra
\mathsf{M}\boxtimes\mathsf{N}
into a factorised algebra
\widetilde{\mathsf{M}}\boxtimes\widetilde{\mathsf{N}}
. Thus, if for instance
\mathcal{G}
would keep factorised the algebra
\langle X\boxtimes X\rangle
but not
\langle X\boxtimes I\rangle
, the statement of Corollary
3
would not be met since
\langle X\boxtimes X\rangle\neq\langle X\rangle\boxtimes\langle X\rangle
. We used the symbol
\langle O\rangle
to denote the algebra generated by the element
O
(see Appendix
A
).

\,\blacksquare

Lemma 11
.

Consider
U\in SU^{1}(2)
, then Eq. (
33
) is satisfied
iff

\displaystyle U=X,Y,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\{X\pm Y\}.

(59)

If this is the case,
\mathsf{M},\,\mathsf{N},\,\widetilde{\mathsf{M}},\,\widetilde{\mathsf{N}}=\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
.

Proof.

The proof is analogous to that of Lemma
10
. We provide the computations for two odd anticommuting generators of
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})\boxtimes\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
, and one can easily retrace the steps of the former proof to obtain that the set of solutions is independent of the choice of generators.

The most general odd unitary
\gamma\in\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
is
\gamma=e^{i\alpha}(\cos\beta X+\sin\beta Y)
, thus if we pick
aX+bY=\gamma\in SU^{1}(2)
then
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(X,Y)(\Sigma\boxtimes I)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[\{\Sigma,X\}\boxtimes Y+(\Sigma-X\Sigma X)\boxtimes I]\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}(X,Y)(I\boxtimes\Sigma)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[-X\boxtimes\{\Sigma,Y\}+I\boxtimes(\Sigma-Y\Sigma Y)]\,,

for
\Sigma=X,Y
. Hence
\mathcal{S}
maps
X\boxtimes I\mapsto I\boxtimes Y
,
Y\boxtimes I\mapsto Y\boxtimes I
,
I\boxtimes X\mapsto I\boxtimes X
, and
I\boxtimes Y\mapsto-X\boxtimes I
.
Computing the action over the generators of
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})\boxtimes\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{G}(\Sigma\boxtimes I)=-\mathcal{S}(X,Y)\{(\gamma^{\dagger}\boxtimes\gamma^{\dagger})\mathcal{S}(X,Y)[\Sigma\boxtimes I](\gamma\boxtimes\gamma)\}\,,\\
&\\
&\mathcal{G}(X\boxtimes I)=-\mathcal{S}(X,Y)[I\boxtimes\gamma Y\gamma]=\\
&=-\mathcal{S}(X,Y)[I\boxtimes(iaZ+bI)(aX+bY)]=\\
&=-\mathcal{S}(X,Y)\{I\boxtimes[(b^{2}-a^{2})Y+2abX]\}=\\
&=(b^{2}-a^{2})X\boxtimes I-2abI\boxtimes X\,,\\
&\\
&\mathcal{G}(Y\boxtimes I)=-\mathcal{S}(X,Y)[\gamma Y\gamma\boxtimes I]=\\
&=-(b^{2}-a^{2})Y\boxtimes I-2abI\boxtimes Y\,,\\
&\\
&\mathcal{G}(I\boxtimes X)=-\mathcal{S}(X,Y)[I\boxtimes\gamma X\gamma]=\\
&=-\mathcal{S}(X,Y)\{I\boxtimes[(a^{2}-b^{2})X+2abY]\}=\\
&=-(a^{2}-b^{2})I\boxtimes X+2abX\boxtimes I\,,\\
&\\
&\mathcal{G}(I\boxtimes Y)=\mathcal{S}(X,Y)[\gamma X\gamma\boxtimes I]=\\
&=(a^{2}-b^{2})I\boxtimes Y+2abY\boxtimes I\,.\end{aligned}

The generators remain factorised iff

\displaystyle\lvert a\rvert=\lvert b\rvert\lor a=0\lor b=0.

(60)

One can prove the same for a generic linear combination of generators. Following the same argument discussed in the proof of Lemma
10
, the only solutions satisfying the conditions of Corollary
3
give
\mathsf{M}\simeq\mathsf{N}\simeq\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
. Plugging the solutions (
60
) in the expression of
G
as in Eq. (
57
) we get

\displaystyle\begin{cases}G=\frac{1}{2}\{X\boxtimes X+Y\boxtimes Y\pm I\boxtimes I\pm Z\boxtimes Z)\,,\\
G=Y\boxtimes Y\,,\\
G=X\boxtimes X\,,\end{cases}

(61)

respectively.
The first expression for
G
in (
61
) corresponds to the operator
\exp{(\pi/4)X\gt X}\exp{(\pi/4)Y\gt Y}=S_{\leftrightarrow}
(or minus its adjoint ), whose conjugation implements the fermionic swap, i.e., in this case
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{S}
(or
-\mathcal{S}^{\dagger}
). Thus, in any case
\mathcal{G}
keeps
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})\boxtimes\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
factorised.

\,\blacksquare

The previous two lemmas
10
,
11
are summarised in the following proposition.

Proposition 5
.

The Forking Automata that satisfy Eq. (
33
) are given by:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} G_{\text{even}}=\begin{cases}X\boxtimes Y\quad\text{if}\quad\theta=n\pi,\\
\\
-Y\boxtimes X\quad\text{if}\quad\theta=\frac{\pi}{2}+n\pi,\\
\\
iS_{\leftrightarrow}^{\dagger}(Z\boxtimes I)\quad\text{if}\quad\theta=\frac{\pi}{4}+\frac{n\pi}{2},\end{cases}\\
\\
G_{\text{odd}}=\begin{cases}X\boxtimes X\quad\text{if}\quad U=X,\\
\\
Y\boxtimes Y\quad\text{if}\quad U=Y,\\
\\
S_{\leftrightarrow}\quad\text{if}\quad U=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\{X\pm Y\}.\end{cases}\end{aligned}

(62)

Proof.

Insert the results of the previous two lemmas in the expression of
G
in Eq. (
9
).

\,\blacksquare

Finally, to get Proposition
4
it is sufficient to apply Proposition
3
to the non-factorised
G
in Eq. (
62
). Proposition
3
states that if
\mathcal{G}
is a fermionic swap up to on-site gates
\mathcal{V}
, then its
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisations can only be shifts with
\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}}=I\boxtimes\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}(\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}\boxtimes I)
where
\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
is an eigenoperator of
\mathcal{V}
. In the present case
\mathcal{V}
acts by conjugation via
V=I,Z
. Although when
V=I
the operator
\outerproduct{\psi}{\psi}
can be any, the only states with definite parity—i.e. well-defined projections in
\mathsf{A}_{x}
—are eigenstates of
\mathcal{Z}
, which leads to the projections
P_{L,R}
in Proposition
4
.

B.3
Majorana Shift

The last class of FCA which is left to analyse are the
Majorana Shifts
, with
\operatorname{ind}(\sigma_{\pm})=2^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}
.

We recall that the action of a Majorana shift is given by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\sigma_{\pm}(X_{x})=Y_{x},\\
&\sigma_{\pm}(Y_{x})=X_{x\pm 1},\end{aligned}

and consequently

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}(Z_{x})=Y_{x}\boxtimes X_{x\pm 1},

possibly followed by a local unitary acting on each cell as
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)=U(\cdot)U^{\dagger}
with
U=\exp\left(-\frac{\theta}{2}iZ\right)
, i.e.

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{\pm}(X_{x})=\xi_{x},\\
\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{\pm}(Y_{x})=\eta_{x\pm 1},\\
\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{\pm}(Z_{x})=\xi_{x}\boxtimes\eta_{x\pm 1},\end{aligned}

for two arbitrary odd anticommuting unitary operators
\xi,\eta
.

Proposition 6
.

The Majorana shift is
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisable
iff

U=\exp\left(-i\frac{2n+1}{2}\pi Z\right)

through the projections:

\Pi^{X}=\frac{I\boxtimes I\pm iX\boxtimes X}{2}\,,\quad\Pi^{Y}=\frac{I\boxtimes I\pm iY\boxtimes Y}{2}.

Those renormalisable Majorana Shifts act as:

\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{\pm}(X_{x})=\mp X_{x}\,,\quad\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{\pm}(Y_{x})=\pm Y_{x\pm 1}.

Proof.

Notice that the only renormalisation condition we can impose on such evolution is the one in Theorem
1
, since no circuital implementation can be invoked to simplify the condition.
We focus, without loss of generality, on the action of the right Majorana shift
\sigma_{+}
.
Two steps of such evolution are given by:

\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+}\circ\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+}.

Let
\mathcal{V}
be the map such that:

\mathcal{V}\circ\sigma_{+}=\sigma_{+}\circ\mathcal{U}.

Since

\sigma_{+}\circ\mathcal{U}(X_{x})=aY_{x}+bX_{x+1}\quad\sigma_{+}\circ\mathcal{U}(Y_{x})=aX_{x+1}-bY_{x},

as, without loss of generality, one can always write
\mathcal{U}(X)=aX+bY
and
\mathcal{U}(Y)=aY-bX
for
a=\cos\theta,b=\sin\theta
with
\theta\in[0,\pi]
7
7

7

Let
\xi=aX+bY
and
\eta=cX+dY
with
a,b,c,d\in\mathbb{C}
. By requiring
\{\xi,\eta\}=0
and
\xi^{2}=\eta^{2}=I
one gets that the matrix of coefficients
\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}
must be orthogonal. Moreover, as
\xi,\eta
are hermitian, the coefficients must be real.
,
we get:

\mathcal{V}(X_{x})=aX_{x}-bY_{x-1}\quad\mathcal{V}(Y_{x})=aY_{x}+bX_{x+1}.

Consider now the renormalisation condition as per Theorem
1

\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+}\circ\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+}(\Pi)=\Pi.

We can recast it as:

\mathcal{U}\circ\mathcal{V}\circ\sigma_{+}^{2}(\Pi)=\Pi,

i.e.

\mathcal{U}\circ\mathcal{V}\circ\tau_{+}(\Pi)=\Pi.

(63)

By composing with
\mathcal{U}^{-1}
on the left of both members of Eq.(
63
) and defining
\Pi^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathcal{U}^{-1}(\Pi)
, we can represent Eq.(
63
) in a diagrammatic way as:

\mathcal{V}\left(\resizebox{7913610}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}.

Let
V=\exp\left(-\frac{\theta}{2}Y\boxtimes X\right)
,
a=\cos\theta,b=\sin\theta
so that

\displaystyle V(X\boxtimes I)V^{\dagger}=X\boxtimes I\,,

\displaystyle V(I\boxtimes X)V^{\dagger}=-bY\boxtimes I+aI\boxtimes X\,,

\displaystyle V(Y\boxtimes I)V^{\dagger}=aY\boxtimes I+bI\boxtimes X\,,

\displaystyle V(I\boxtimes Y)V^{\dagger}=I\boxtimes Y.

We have the diagrammatic equation:

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
}}.

(64)

Comparing the factorisations on the left and right of Eq.(
64
), we obtain the following necessary and sufficient conditions for our evolution to be renormalised:

\begin{split}\mathcal{V}_{\text{loc}}(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}})=P_{1}\boxtimes P_{2},\\
\mathcal{V}_{\text{loc}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{U}^{-1}(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}})=P_{2}\boxtimes P_{1},\end{split}

(65)

i.e. the image of
\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}
under
\mathcal{V}_{\text{loc}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\text{loc}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{U}^{-1}
should be factorised, and equal up to a fermionic swap

SWAP\circ\mathcal{V}_{\text{loc}}(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}})=\mathcal{V}_{\text{loc}}^{-1}\circ\mathcal{U}^{-1}(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}).

In general we can write any rank-2 projection as
\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}=\frac{1}{2}\{I\boxtimes I+T_{\text{less}}\}
, where
T_{\text{less}}
is a traceless operator. Then the only possible solutions of Eq. (
65
) are:

P_{1}\boxtimes P_{2}=\frac{(I\pm Z)\boxtimes I}{2},\frac{I\boxtimes(I\pm Z)}{2},

where we considered
(P_{1}\boxtimes P_{2})^{2}=P_{1}\boxtimes P_{2}
and the parity superselection (
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading of the algebra). We explicitly verify the claim below.
Since
\text{rank}(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}})=2
then in order for
P_{2}\boxtimes P_{2}
to have the same rank as
\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}
it must be
P_{1}\boxtimes P_{2}=I\boxtimes P_{\pm}
(or
=P_{\pm}\boxtimes I
), where
P_{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}(I\pm Z)
. Hence, the only possible solutions for Eqs. (
65
) are the ones listed above.

We compute
\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}=\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{loc}(P_{1}\boxtimes P_{2})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{loc}(\frac{1}{2}(I\pm Z)\boxtimes I)=\frac{1}{2}(I\boxtimes I\pm(aZ\boxtimes I+ibX\boxtimes X))

(66)

\mathcal{V}^{-1}_{loc}(\frac{1}{2}I\boxtimes(I\pm Z))=\frac{1}{2}(I\boxtimes I\pm(aI\boxtimes Z-ibY\boxtimes Y))

and
\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}=\mathcal{U}\circ\mathcal{V}_{loc}(P_{2}\boxtimes P_{1})
:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{U}\circ\mathcal{V}_{loc}(\frac{1}{2}I\boxtimes(I\pm Z))=\\
&=\frac{1}{2}(I\boxtimes I\pm(aI\boxtimes Z-ib\mathcal{U}(Y)\boxtimes\mathcal{U}(Y)))=\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\{I\boxtimes I\pm[aI\boxtimes Z+\\
&-ib[a^{2}Y\boxtimes Y-ab(Y\boxtimes X+X\boxtimes Y)+b^{2}X\boxtimes X]]\}\end{aligned}

(67)

As Eq.s(
66
) and (
67
) must be equal, it follows
a=0
and
b^{3}=b
, i.e.
\theta=\pm\pi/2+k\pi
.

Therefore the only
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisable right Majorana shifts are
\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+}(X_{x})=\mp X_{x},\,\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+}(Y_{x})=\pm Y_{x+1}
. Analogously, the only
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisable left Majorana shifts are
\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{-}(X_{x})=\mp X_{x},\,\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{-}(Y_{x})=\pm Y_{x-1}
.

\,\blacksquare

The
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalised FCA of Majorana shifts are summarised in the following Proposition.

Proposition 7
.

The
(2,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisable Majorana Shifts
\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+}
are renormalised in a shift through
\Pi^{X}
and into a trivial FCA through
\Pi^{Y}
.

Proof.

We first show that the Majorana shift is renormalised into a shift through
\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}=\Pi^{X}
.
The algebra

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}^{X}(\mathsf{A}({\Lambda_{x}}))=\Pi^{X}\mathsf{A}({\Lambda_{x}})\Pi^{X}\boxtimes\bigboxtimes_{x\neq y\in\mathbb{L^{\prime}}}\Pi_{\Lambda_{y}}\cong\mathsf{A}^{\prime}_{x}\boxtimes\mathbb{C}\mathcal{I}

(68)

is isomorphic to
\operatorname{Mat}(\mathbb{C}^{1\lvert 1})
with two odd graded-commuting generators given by

\displaystyle\xi_{1,\Lambda_{x}}=\mathcal{P}^{X}(I_{2x}\boxtimes Y_{2x+1})\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{2,\Lambda_{x}}=\mathcal{P}^{X}(Y_{2x}\boxtimes I_{2x+1})\,,

where
\xi_{j,\Lambda_{x}}\equiv\mathcal{E}(\xi_{j,x}^{\prime})
with
\xi_{j,x}^{\prime}
two odd graded-commuting generators of
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}_{x}
. The isomorphism in Eq.(
68
) is realised by
\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
as per Eq.(
18
). We recall that the renormalised FCA
\mathcal{S}
is given by
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ(\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+})^{2}\circ\mathcal{E}
and for a renormalisable FCA we have
(\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+})^{2}\circ\mathcal{P}^{X}=\mathcal{P}^{X}\circ(\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+})^{2}
, with
\mathcal{P}^{X}=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}})=I_{x}\in\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
. Now, since
(\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+})^{2}(Y_{x})=Y_{x+2}
, we have

\displaystyle(\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+})^{2}\circ\mathcal{E}(\xi^{\prime}_{1,x})=(\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+})^{2}\circ\mathcal{P}(I_{2x}\boxtimes Y_{2x+1})=

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}\circ(\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+})^{2}(I_{2x}\boxtimes Y_{2x+1})=

\displaystyle=(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}\boxtimes\xi_{1,\Lambda_{x+1}})\boxtimes\bigboxtimes_{y\in\mathbb{L^{\prime}}\setminus\{x,x+1\}}\Pi_{\Lambda_{y}}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}\boxtimes\xi_{1,\Lambda_{x+1}}\boxtimes\bigboxtimes_{y\in\mathbb{L^{\prime}}\setminus\{x,x+1\}}\Pi_{\Lambda_{y}})=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I\boxtimes\xi^{\prime}_{1,x+1}\,.

Repeating the same steps for
\xi_{2,x}^{\prime}
, we get
\mathcal{S}(\xi_{2,x}^{\prime})=I\boxtimes\xi^{\prime}_{2,x+1}
.

By noticing that
(\mathcal{U}\circ\sigma_{+})^{2}(X_{x})=X_{x}
, one can easily show that the Majorana shift is renormalised into one layer of cell-wise gates through
\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}=\Pi^{Y}
by applying the same above argument using as generators of
\mathcal{P}^{Y}(\mathsf{A}({\Lambda_{x}}))

\displaystyle\xi_{1,\Lambda_{x}}=\mathcal{P}^{Y}(I_{2x}\boxtimes X_{2x+1})\,,

\displaystyle\xi_{2,\Lambda_{x}}=\mathcal{P}^{Y}(X_{2x}\boxtimes I_{2x+1})\,.

\,\blacksquare

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
1

In this appendix we go through essentially the same proofs presented in
[
18
]
for quantum cellular automata. We show that those results can be extended to the case of
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebras.

We define the map
\mathcal{T}_{r}
induced by
\mathcal{T}_{w}
on
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L^{\prime}})
as follows.

Definition 36

(Induced map)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a QCA on
\mathbb{Z}^{s}
,
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{Z}_{Nm}^{s}

be a regular wrapping,
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}=\mathbb{Z}_{m}^{s}
a coarse-grained lattice,
and let
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
and
\mathcal{E}
be defined as in Eq. (
18
).
The
induced map

\mathcal{T}_{r}:\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L^{\prime}})\to\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L^{\prime}})
is defined as

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{T}_{r}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{T}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E}.\end{aligned}

(69)

Notice that we have a

family
of induced maps

\{\mathcal{T}_{r}\}_{\mathbb{L},\mathbb{L^{\prime}}}
depending on the choice of the
lattices
\mathbb{L}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
. To simplify notation, we omit this dependence, since the transition rule is ultimately independent of their choice, as we will prove later.
We remark that, in general, we need to differentiate between the map induced by
N

steps of the FCA
\mathcal{T}
and
N
subsequent applications of the map induced by
\mathcal{T}
.
These two transformations are given by

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{T}_{w}^{N}\circ\mathcal{E}

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}_{r})^{N}=(\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{T}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E})^{N},

(70)

respectively.
In the following we will see how the induced map pins down the idea of coarse-graining.

Lemma 12
.

If a FCA
\mathcal{T}
of
\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{Z}^{s})
is

(N,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})-
renormalisable to some FCA
\mathcal{S}
,
then every induced map
(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}
is an FCA. In particular,
(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}=\mathcal{S}_{w}
.

Proof.

The induced map of
N
steps of
\mathcal{T}
is

(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E}.

(71)

Composing Eq. (
19
) on the left with
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
we have

\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{S}_{w}=\mathcal{S}_{w},

whence

\mathcal{S}_{w}=(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r},

for every possible choice of regular wrapping
\mathbb{L}
.

\,\blacksquare

Notice that, if
(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}
is an FCA for every
choice of the lattice
\mathbb{L}
, the Wrapping
Lemma allows us to extend it to an FCA
\mathcal{S}
on the
quasi-local algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}^{s})
over the infinite
lattice built from the local algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
. However, the transition rule of
\mathcal{S}
and the algebra
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
may, in principle, depend on the specific wrapping.
In the following, we prove that this is not the case.

Let
\mathcal{T}:\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{Z}^{s})\to\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{Z}^{s})
and
\mathcal{S}:\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}^{s})\to\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{Z}^{s})
be two FCA, and let us study when
condition (
19
) holds for a given
wrapping
\mathbb{L}
of
\mathbb{Z}^{s}
. Since
\mathcal{T}_{w}

is a graded
*
-automorphism of a central simple finite-dimensional
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra, by the
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded counterpart of the Skolem-Noether Theorem (Appendix
A
),
we can
always find a unitary
U_{\mathcal{T}}\in\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{L})
such that

\mathcal{T}_{w}(A)=(U_{\mathcal{T}})^{\dagger}A(U_{\mathcal{T}})\hskip 10.0pt\forall A\in\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{L}),

(72)

and the same holds for
\mathcal{S}_{w}

\mathcal{S}_{w}(A)=(U_{\mathcal{S}})^{\dagger}A(U_{\mathcal{S}})\hskip 10.0pt\forall A\in\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime}).

Moreover, Eq.(
71
) allows us to write

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}(A)=(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}^{\dagger}A(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}\,,

(73)

\displaystyle(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}\coloneqq\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})\,,

(74)

where in principle
(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}
might not be a unitary operator. However, condition (
19
) implies that
(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}=U_{\mathcal{S}}e^{i\phi}
is a unitary operator for every regular wrapping
\mathbb{L}
. If this is the case, Eq.(
73
) guarantees that
(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}
is a graded
*
-automorphism.

The following Lemma identifies a necessary and sufficient condition on
U_{\mathcal{T}}
for Eq.(
19
) to hold.

Lemma 13
.

Eq.(
19
) holds with
\mathcal{S}_{w}=\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}
being a graded
*
-automorphism iff for all regular wrappings

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}(\Pi)=\Pi,

(75)

or equivalently

\left[U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}},\,\Pi\right]=0\,.

(76)

Proof.

If (
19
) holds, we can compose it on the right with
\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
and get

\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{S}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}.

(77)

Now, composing the r.h.s. of the latter expression with
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}
on the left gives

\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{S}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{S}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{P},

while on the other hand, considering l.h.s., we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{P}.

(78)

Evaluating Eq.(
78
) on the identity element we get
\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\Pi=U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}
,
where
U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}
is the unitary associated with
\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}
.
Since
\Pi^{\prime}\coloneqq U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}
is a projection such that
\Pi\Pi^{\prime}\Pi=\Pi^{\prime}
, then
\Pi\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}\Pi=\Pi^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{rank}(\Pi^{\prime})=\operatorname{rank}(\Pi)
. Thus we have
\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi
, i.e.

\displaystyle U^{\dagger}_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}=\Pi\,,

which is Eq.(
75
).
This amounts to the commutation condition (
76
). Moreover, this condition is equivalent to

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{T}^{N}=\mathcal{T}^{N}\circ\mathcal{P}\,.

(79)

Indeed, on the one hand Eq. (
79
) clearly implies
\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{P}
; on the other hand from Eq. (
75
) for any
O\in\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{L})
we have
\Pi O\Pi=\mathcal{P}(O)=U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}OU_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}=\mathcal{T}^{N}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ(\mathcal{T}^{N})^{-1}(O)
.

To prove the converse,
we remind that
(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})
,
Then, exploiting (
50
), we have

\displaystyle(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}^{\dagger}=\sum_{f,g,l,k\in S}\mathcal{J}_{f\psi_{f},g\psi_{g}}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})\mathcal{J}_{l\psi_{l},k\psi_{k}}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger})=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(\Pi)=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{E}(I_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})})=I_{\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})},

where we exploited the commutation of
U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}

with
\Pi
. The same holds for

(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}
and thus
(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}
is
unitary over
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L^{\prime}})
. We can thus make the
identification
U_{\mathcal{S}}=(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}
, and use the fact that an isometry is a
*
-homomorphism to obtain
the identity

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{S}_{w}(X)=\mathcal{E}((U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r}^{\dagger})\mathcal{E}(X)\mathcal{E}((U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})_{r})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger})\mathcal{E}(X)\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger})\mathcal{E}(X)\mathcal{P}(U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}})

\displaystyle=\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi\mathcal{E}(X)\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\Pi

\displaystyle=U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi\mathcal{E}(X)\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{P}\circ\mathcal{E}(X)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{E}(X),

which finally yields

\mathcal{E}\circ\mathcal{S}_{w}=\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}\circ\mathcal{E},

with
\mathcal{S}_{w}=\mathcal{V}
an automorphism of the algebra

\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
.

\,\blacksquare

Notice that Eq. (
75
) is necessary and sufficient for condition (
19
) for
some
automorphism
\mathcal{V}
, which by now is not necessarily the wrapped version of a given FCA
\mathcal{S}
on
\mathbb{Z}^{s}
. Thus, we do not have yet a necessary and sufficient condition for renormalisability.

We now show that all
maps
\mathcal{V}
share the same transition rule. This has
two important consequences. The first one is that Eqs. (
71
)
and (
75
) become necessary and sufficient for
(N,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisability. The second and most powerful one is that we can check the
condition in Eq.(
75
) only on
the smallest regular wrapping for

\mathcal{T}^{N}
. We first provide two preliminary lemmas.

Lemma 14
.

(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}
is invariant under shifts on
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

A shift
\tau_{x}^{w}
along the vector
x\in\mathbb{L}^{\prime}

corresponds to a shift
\tau_{Nx}^{w}

along the vector
Nx\in\mathbb{L}
.
Since

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \tau_{Nx}^{w}\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}\tau_{x}^{w},\\
\tau_{x}^{w}\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}=\mathcal{E}^{\dagger}\tau_{Nx}^{w},\\
\end{aligned}

and the FCA

\mathcal{T}^{N}
commutes with
\tau_{Nx}^{w}
,
by their composition for the induced map
(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}
it follows

\displaystyle[(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r},\tau_{x}]=0\quad\forall x\in\mathbb{L}^{\prime}.

\,\blacksquare

Lemma 15
.

U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}
can be written in the following form:

\displaystyle U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}=[(I_{\Lambda_{0}}\boxtimes V)(U\boxtimes I_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{W}}})],

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\mathbb{L}\setminus(\Lambda_{0}\cup\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}),

where
U
is independent of the wrapping, and
\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}
denotes the neighbourhood of
\Lambda_{0}
for
\mathcal{T}^{N}
.

Proof.

By definition of FCA
\mathcal{T}(\mathsf{A}(\Lambda_{0}))\subseteq\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}})
, then
\mathcal{T}^{N}(\mathsf{A}(\Lambda_{0}))\subseteq\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}})
.
In other words, the algebra
\mathcal{T}^{N}(\mathsf{A}(\Lambda_{0}))
is an homomorphic representation of the algebra
\mathsf{A}(\Lambda_{0})
in

\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}})
, which means that there exists a unitary

U\in\mathsf{A}(\Lambda_{0}\cup\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}})
such that, for every

O\in\mathsf{A}(\Lambda_{0})

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{N}(O)=U^{\dagger}(O\boxtimes I_{\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}\setminus\Lambda_{0}})U\in\mathsf{A}(\Lambda_{0}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}).

For any
B\in\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{L})
the wrapped evolution
\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}
is implemented over the wrapping by a unitary operator (Eq. (
72
))

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{N}_{w}(B)=U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}(B\boxtimes I_{\mathbb{L}\setminus\mathcal{N}_{B}})U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}.

(80)

Since
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{T}_{w}
share the same transition rule, we then have

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{w}^{N}(B)

\displaystyle=U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}(B\boxtimes I_{\mathbb{L}\setminus\Lambda_{0}})U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}

\displaystyle=U^{\dagger}(B\boxtimes I_{\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}}\setminus\Lambda_{0}})U\boxtimes I_{\mathbb{L}\setminus(\Lambda_{0}\cup\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}})}.

One can easily prove that this implies

U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}(U\boxtimes I_{\mathbb{L}\setminus(\Lambda_{0}\cup\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}})})^{\dagger}=(I_{\Lambda_{0}}\boxtimes V),

for some unitary
U\in\mathsf{A}(\mathbb{L}\setminus\Lambda_{0})
, and finally

U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}=(I_{\Lambda_{0}}\boxtimes V)(U\boxtimes I_{\mathbb{L}\setminus(\Lambda_{0}\cup\mathcal{N}^{N}_{\Lambda_{0}})}).

(81)

\,\blacksquare

We now prove that the condition (
75
) is independent of the wrapping.

Lemma 16
.

If condition in (
75
) is satisfied for a regular wrapping
\mathbb{L}
for
\mathcal{T}^{N}
, then it is true for every other regular wrapping.

Proof.

Let us divide the projection
\Pi
over
\mathbb{L}
in

\begin{split}&\Pi=\Pi_{0}\boxtimes\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{W}},\\
&{\mathcal{M}}\coloneqq\mathcal{N}\setminus\{0\},\\
&\mathcal{W}\coloneqq\mathbb{L}\setminus(\mathcal{N}\cup\{0\}),\end{split}

with the convention:

\displaystyle\Pi_{{R}}=\left(\bigboxtimes_{x\in R}\Pi_{{x}}\right).

The commutation relation (
75
) can then be expressed as

\begin{split}&U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}(\Pi_{0}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{W}})U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}=\Pi_{0}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{W}}.\end{split}

(82)

We can represent Eq. (
82
) diagrammatically, with the decomposition (
81
) of
U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}
, as follows

(83)

Exploiting the decompositon in Eq.(
81
) and conjugating both members of Eq. (
82
) with
(I_{\Lambda_{0}}\boxtimes V)^{\dagger}
we get to:

\begin{split}&(U\boxtimes I_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{W}}})(\Pi_{0}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{W}})(U\boxtimes I_{\Lambda_{\mathcal{W}}})^{\dagger}=\\
&=(I_{\Lambda_{0}}\boxtimes V)^{\dagger}(\Pi_{0}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{W}})(I_{\Lambda_{0}}\boxtimes V).\end{split}

(84)

The two members of Eq. (
84
) are factorised in two different ways. This implies that both factorisations must hold, and then
the transformation
V
on the right produces a factorised projection map, i.e.

\begin{split}&(I_{0}\boxtimes V)^{\dagger}(\Pi_{0}\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathcal{M}}\boxtimes\Pi_{{\mathcal{W}}})(I_{0}\boxtimes V)=\\
&=\Pi_{0}\boxtimes Z^{\dagger}\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}Z\boxtimes\Pi_{{\mathcal{W}}},\end{split}

for some unitary
Z\in\mathsf{A}(\mathcal{M})
. In particular, we have the following diagrammatic identity

{\hbox to43.68pt{\vbox to50.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.45845pt\lower-6.19055pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

The latter is independent of the lattice
\mathbb{L}
, and proves that the image of

\Pi
under
U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}\rvert_{\Pi}
is a projection of the form

\widetilde{\Pi}_{{\mathbb{L}^{\prime}\setminus\{0\}}}\boxtimes\Pi_{0}
. Finally, by translation
invariance we can conclude that for every regular wrapping
\mathbb{L}
for
\mathcal{T}^{N}
one has

\displaystyle U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}=\Pi\,.

\,\blacksquare

This immediately implies the following corollary.

Corollary 5
.

If condition in (
75
) is satisfied for a regular wrapping
\mathbb{L}
for
\mathcal{T}^{N}
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&(U\boxtimes I_{\mathcal{W}})^{\dagger}(\Pi_{0}\boxtimes Z^{\dagger}\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}Z\boxtimes\Pi_{{\mathcal{W}}})(U\boxtimes I_{\mathcal{W}})\\
&=\Pi_{0}\boxtimes\Pi_{{\mathcal{M}}}\boxtimes\Pi_{{\mathcal{W}}}.\end{split}

(85)

The above result allows us to show that condition (
75
) is necessary and sufficient for
(N,\Pi_{\Lambda_{0}})
-renormalisability.

Lemma 17
.

Let
\mathbb{L}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}
be two regular wrappings for
\mathcal{T}^{N}
, and
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime}
the associated coarse-grained lattices. Let condition (
75
) be satisfied for both. Then the transition rule of the induced map
(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}
over

\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime})
is the same.

Proof.

It suffices to notice that a local operator
A_{x}
in
\mathsf{A}^{\prime}(\mathbb{L}^{\prime})\cong\mathsf{A}^{\Pi}(\mathbb{L})
is obtained as
\Pi A_{\Lambda_{x}}\Pi
for some
A_{\Lambda_{x}}
on the macro-cell

\Lambda_{x}
. As a consequence, one has

\displaystyle U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}A_{x}U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}=U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}^{\dagger}\Pi A_{\Lambda_{x}}\Pi U_{\mathcal{T}^{N}}

\displaystyle\quad=U^{\dagger}(\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}A_{\Lambda_{x}}\Pi_{\Lambda_{x}}\boxtimes Z^{\dagger}\Pi_{\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{x}}\setminus\Lambda_{x}}Z)U\boxtimes\Pi_{\mathbb{L}\setminus(\Lambda_{x}\cup\mathcal{N}_{\Lambda_{x}})}),

where
U
is independent of
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}
. Finally, by Eq. (
73
) and (
74
), one has

\displaystyle(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}\rvert_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime}}(A_{x})=(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}\rvert_{\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}(A_{x}),

for every
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime}
, where we used
(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}\rvert_{\mathbb{G}}
to denote the FCA
(\mathcal{T}^{N})_{r}
restricted to the particular lattice
\mathbb{G}
.

\,\blacksquare
